</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abstract1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<section id="sec1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Background:</h3>
<p>Exposure to traffic-related air pollution (TRAP) has been associated with increased risks of respiratory diseases, but the biological mechanisms are not yet fully elucidated.</p></section><section id="sec2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Objectives:</h3>
<p>Our aim was to evaluate the respiratory responses and explore potential biological mechanisms of TRAP exposure in a randomized crossover trial.</p></section><section id="sec3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Methods:</h3>
<p>We conducted a randomized crossover trial in 56 healthy adults. Each participant was exposed to high- and low-TRAP exposure sessions by walking in a park and down a road with high traffic volume for 4 h in random order. Respiratory symptoms and lung function, including forced expiratory volume in the first second (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i1" display="inline" alttext="uppercase f e v begin subscript 1 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mtext>FEV</mtext></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>), forced vital capacity (FVC), the ratio of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i2" display="inline" alttext="uppercase f e v begin subscript 1 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mtext>FEV</mtext></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> to FVC, and maximal mid-expiratory flow (MMEF), were measured before and after each exposure session. Markers of 8-isoprostane, tumor necrosis <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i3" display="inline" alttext="factor lowercase alpha"><mrow><mtext>factor-</mtext><mi mathvariant="normal">α</mi></mrow></math></span> (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i4" display="inline" alttext="uppercase t n f lowercase alpha"><mrow><mtext>TNF-</mtext><mi mathvariant="normal">α</mi></mrow></math></span>), and ezrin in exhaled breath condensate (EBC), and surfactant proteins D (SP-D) in serum were also measured. We used linear mixed-effects models to estimate the associations, adjusted for age, sex, body mass index, meteorological condition, and batch (only for biomarkers). Liquid chromatography–mass spectrometry was used to profile the EBC metabolome. Untargeted metabolome-wide association study (MWAS) analysis and pathway enrichment analysis using mummichog were performed to identify critical metabolomic features and pathways associated with TRAP exposure.</p></section><section id="sec4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Results:</h3>
<p>Participants had two to three times higher exposure to traffic-related air pollutants except for fine particulate matter while walking along the road compared with in the park. Compared with the low-TRAP exposure at the park, high-TRAP exposure at the road was associated with a higher score of respiratory symptoms [2.615 (95% CI: 0.605, 4.626), <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i5" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p equals 1.2 times 10 begin superscript negative 2 end superscript"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1.2</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mrow><mrow><mn>10</mn></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></mrow></msup></mrow></math></span>] and relatively lower lung function indicators [<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i6" display="inline" alttext="negative 0.075 liter"><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>0.075</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">L</mi></mrow></math></span> (95% CI: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i7" display="inline" alttext="negative 0.138"><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>0.138</mn></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i8" display="inline" alttext="negative 0.012"><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>0.012</mn></mrow></math></span>), <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i9" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p equals 1.2 times 10 begin superscript negative 2 end superscript"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>2.1</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mrow><mrow><mn>10</mn></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></mrow></msup></mrow></math></span>] for <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i10" display="inline" alttext="uppercase f e v begin subscript 1 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mtext>FEV</mtext></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i11" display="inline" alttext="negative 0.190 liter per second"><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>0.190</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">L</mi><mo>/</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi></mrow></math></span> (95% CI: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i12" display="inline" alttext="negative 0.351"><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>0.351</mn></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i13" display="inline" alttext="negative 0.029"><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>0.029</mn></mrow></math></span>; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i14" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p equals 2.4 times 10 begin superscript negative 2 end superscript"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>2.4</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mrow><mrow><mn>10</mn></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></mrow></msup></mrow></math></span>) for MMEF]. Exposure to TRAP was significantly associated with changes in some, but not all, biomarkers, particularly with a <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i15" display="inline" alttext="0.494 nanogram per milliliter"><mrow><mn>0.494</mn><mtext>-ng</mtext><mo>/</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">mL</mi></mrow></math></span> (95% CI: 0.297, 0.691; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i16" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p equals 9.5 times 10 begin superscript negative 6 end superscript"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>9.5</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mrow><mrow><mn>10</mn></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>6</mn></mrow></mrow></msup></mrow></math></span>) increase for serum SP-D and a <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i17" display="inline" alttext="0.123 nanogram per milliliter"><mrow><mn>0.123</mn><mtext>-ng</mtext><mo>/</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">mL</mi></mrow></math></span> (95% CI: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i18" display="inline" alttext="negative 0.208"><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>0.208</mn></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i19" display="inline" alttext="negative 0.037"><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>0.037</mn></mrow></math></span>; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i20" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p equals 7.2 times 10 begin superscript negative 3 end superscript"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>7.2</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mrow><mrow><mn>10</mn></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow></mrow></msup></mrow></math></span>) decrease for EBC ezrin. Untargeted MWAS analysis revealed that elevated TRAP exposure was significantly associated with perturbations in 23 and 32 metabolic pathways under positive- and negative-ion modes, respectively. These pathways were most related to inflammatory response, oxidative stress, and energy use metabolism.</p></section><section id="sec5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Conclusions:</h3>
<p>This study suggests that TRAP exposure might lead to lung function impairment and respiratory symptoms. Possible underlying mechanisms include lung epithelial injury, inflammation, oxidative stress, and energy metabolism disorders. <a href="https://doi.org/10.1289/EHP11139" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://doi.org/10.1289/EHP11139</a></p></section></section><section id="s1"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Introduction</h2>
<p>Respiratory disease is a major contributor to the global burden of disease.<sup><a href="#c1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c1">1</a></sup> Ambient air pollution, especially, has been recognized as an important risk factor for respiratory health.<sup><a href="#c2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c2">2</a>–<a href="#c5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c5">5</a></sup> Traffic emission is the major sour ce of urban air pollution and exposure to traffic-related air pollution (TRAP) has been associated with various respiratory diseases worldwide.<sup><a href="#c6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c6">6</a>–<a href="#c8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c8">8</a></sup> However, most existing studies on air pollution and respiratory morbidity and mortality have been observational and thus had limited ability to establish a causal relationship between exposure and outcome.<sup><a href="#c9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c9">9</a>,<a href="#c10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c10">10</a></sup> Many studies have been conducted under controlled-exposure experimental settings (e.g., by using exposure chambers to create TRAP exposure contrast among the participants).<sup><a href="#c11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c11">11</a>–<a href="#c14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c14">14</a></sup> However, most of these studies considered only one or two pollutants. Meanwhile, in reality, TRAP is a mixture of air pollutants and the main components include nitrogen oxides (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i21" display="inline" alttext="nitrogen oxide"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">NO</mi></mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>), carbon monoxide (CO), fine and ultrafine particulate matter [PM <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i22" display="inline" alttext="less than or equal to 2.5 micrometers"><mrow><mo>≤</mo><mn>2.5</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi></mrow></math></span> in aerodynamic diameter (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i23" display="inline" alttext="particulate matter begin subscript 2.5 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">PM</mi></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>2.5</mn></mrow></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>) and UFP], and black carbon (BC).<sup><a href="#c15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c15">15</a>–<a href="#c17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c17">17</a></sup> Two studies in London, UK, have examined respiratory and cardiovascular responses to TRAP by comparing participants’ health measures after walking down a busy road and in a traffic-free area.<sup><a href="#c18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c18">18</a>,<a href="#c19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c19">19</a></sup> However, their findings may not be generalizable in other countries or regions owing to the differences in vehicle fuel composition, levels of exposure, and population characteristics.</p>
<p>The underlying mechanisms of respiratory effects caused by TRAP have not been fully clarified. The metabolomics technique has emerged as a powerful tool to detect molecular changes following perturbations, such as environmental exposures, comprehensively. However, few studies have fully applied this technique to examine the molecular changes with TRAP exposure.<sup><a href="#c20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c20">20</a>–<a href="#c22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c22">22</a></sup> In addition, only one study has considered profiling metabolites in exhaled breath condensate (EBC) samples to explore changes in the respiratory system.<sup><a href="#c23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c23">23</a></sup> Therefore, more evidence is warranted.</p>
<p>To address these knowledge gaps, we conducted a randomized, crossover study in Shanghai, China, to examine the associations of the respiratory effects associated with TRAP exposure and to explore the underlying mechanisms by analyzing the metabolome in EBC.</p></section><section id="s2"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Methods</h2>
<section id="s2.1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Study Design and Participants</h3>
<p>We initially recruited 69 healthy, nonsmoking college students (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i24" display="inline" alttext="greater than 18"><mrow><mo>&gt;</mo><mn>18</mn></mrow></math></span> years of age) from the medical campus of Fudan University in Shanghai, China. Students who had no history of self-reported allergy or chronic cardiopulmonary diseases, who had lived on campus for at least 1 y, who had no secondhand smoking exposure in their main indoor environments (i.e., dormitory and office), and who had not used any medication or dietary supplements in the recent 2 months were eligible to enroll. We measured their height and weight for calculating body mass index (BMI) and collected their demographic and medical information through a questionnaire that included age, sex, smoking, history of disease (e.g., cardiorespiratory and allergic diseases, major surgeries), and medication and dietary supplement use in the recent 2 months. The questionnaire survey can be found in the Supplemental Materials, “Basic Information Questionnaire.” In addition, we conducted pulmonary function tests for all participants at enrollment to screen for those with abnormal lung function or inability in test performing. The pulmonary function test was conducted according to the recommendation of the American Thoracic Society/European Respiratory Society (ATS/ERS)<sup><a href="#c24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c24">24</a></sup> using a portable spirometer (Jaeger MasterscreenV5.01, CareFusion). Six participants were then excluded because their lung function test results were below the normal range [i.e., the forced expiratory volume in the first second (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i25" display="inline" alttext="uppercase f e v begin subscript 1 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mtext>FEV</mtext></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>) to forced vital capacity (FVC) ratio was <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i26" display="inline" alttext="less than 0.65"><mrow><mo>&lt;</mo><mn>0.65</mn></mrow></math></span> or the predicted <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i27" display="inline" alttext="uppercase f e v begin subscript 1 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mtext>FEV</mtext></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> was <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i28" display="inline" alttext="less than 75 percent"><mrow><mo>&lt;</mo><mn>75</mn><mo>%</mo></mrow></math></span>] and another 7 participants declined to participate. Eventually, 56 participants were included in the trial.</p>
<p>Each participant was required to finish a high-and a low-TRAP exposure session in random orders intermitted by no less than a 14-d washout period between October and December 2019 (Figure S1). For the high-TRAP exposure session, participants were assigned to walk on the sidewalk along North Caoxi Road, which is located in urban Shanghai and has <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i29" display="inline" alttext="approximately 77,400"><mrow><mo>∼</mo><mn>77,400</mn></mrow></math></span> vehicles on the road per day. For the low-exposure session, the participants were led to walk in Century Park following a preassigned walking route <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i30" display="inline" alttext="0.5 to 1.5 kilometers"><mrow><mn>0.5</mn><mo>–</mo><mn>1.5</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">km</mi></mrow></math></span> away from traffic roadways.<sup><a href="#c25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c25">25</a></sup> For feasibility, participants were divided randomly into 14 groups (1–3 persons per group) and each group was arranged to complete the exposure sessions on different days. In each session, participants were asked to walk at a steady pace for 15 min followed by a 30-min rest for 4 h (from 1300 to 1700 hours). To minimize noise exposure, we provided earplugs during each walking session.</p>
<p>To minimize the differential exposure to TRAP prior to each session, all the participants were asked to stay on campus for at least 3 d before each session. To reduce the influence of other potential confounders, all the participants were asked to provide information that might cause short-term systemic inflammation, including disease conditions, use of alcohol, medication and dietary supplements, and passive smoking, in the past 3 d prior to each exposure session by questionnaire (Supplemental Materials, “Information before each exposure session”). Only those who had not taken medication, dietary supplements, consumed alcohol, been exposed to secondhand smoke, or had symptoms of illness within the past 3 d were allowed to participate in the upcoming exposure session. To minimize possible impacts from the diet, we also provided standardized meals and water to all participants on the day of the exposure session.</p>
<p>The study protocol was registered at ClinicalTrials.gov (<a href="https://clinicaltrials.gov/ct2/show/NCT04153539" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">NCT04153539</a>). The institutional review board of the School of Public Health, Fudan University, approved the study protocol (IRB#2019-07-0768). All participants provided written informed consent at enrollment.</p></section><section id="s2.2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Exposure Measures</h3>
<p>During each exposure session, a trained staff member carried a backpack equipped with portable devices to measure real-time air pollution exposures. <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i31" display="inline" alttext="particulate matter begin subscript 2.5 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">PM</mi></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>2.5</mn></mrow></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> mass concentration was measured by MicroPEM (RTI International), which is a portable one-stage impactor equipped with an onboard micro-nephelometer. UFP number concentration was measured by NanoTracer (XP; Oxility), which records the number concentrations of particles with a size between <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i32" display="inline" alttext="20 nanometers"><mrow><mn>20</mn><mtext> nm</mtext></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i33" display="inline" alttext="120 nanometers"><mrow><mn>120</mn><mtext> nm</mtext></mrow></math></span> every 10 s using the diffusion charging method. BC concentration was measured using MicroAeth (AE51; AethLabs), which detects the transmission of light at the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i34" display="inline" alttext="88 nanometer"><mrow><mn>880</mn><mtext>-nm</mtext></mrow></math></span> wavelength through the active area of a filter (referred to as the sensing channel) of aerosol collection. Nitrogen dioxide (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i35" display="inline" alttext="nitrogen dioxide"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">NO</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>) and CO were measured by dynamic baseline electrochemical sensors based on electrochemical sensing and pair differential filter technology, respectively (Sapiens PEK Lite A1; Sapiens Environmental Technology Co. Ltd.).<sup><a href="#c26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c26">26</a></sup> The meteorological variables, including ambient temperature and relative humidity, were also collected using a HOBO data logger (Onset Computer Corporation). The 4-h average of pollutant concentrations for each exposure session was calculated for the statistical analysis.</p>
<p>All devices were tested for comparability and calibrated for data quality control. We conducted side-by-side tests on devices for each air pollutant measurement under laboratory conditions to evaluate the reproducibility within units in a scenario without any major indoor pollutant sources. For each type of device, all the units were placed on a table at the center of the lab (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i36" display="inline" alttext="6 meters by 5 meters by 3 meters"><mrow><mn>6</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi><mo>×</mo><mn>5</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi><mo>×</mo><mn>3</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi></mrow></math></span>) and set to run for a continuous 24 h with a fixed data acquirement interval (i.e., 5 min for NanoTracer, 1 min for MicroPEM, 5 min for MicroAeth, and 1 min for Sapiens PEK Lite). We kept all the windows and doors closed to minimize potential influence from airflow disturbance. We conducted tests for comparability of devices three times in total, that is, before the trial (September 2019), at the middle of the trial (November 2019), and at the end of the trial (December 2019). To reduce the exposure misclassification, two units showing the best interconsistency in the comparability tests were used in the trial (Figures S2–S4).</p></section><section id="s2.3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Health Measurements</h3>
<section id="s2.3.1"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Respiratory symptoms.</h4>
<p>We used a questionnaire adapted from the Swedish Performance Evaluation System (SPES)<sup><a href="#c27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c27">27</a></sup> to measure the degree of respiratory symptoms. This questionnaire requires participants to use a scale of 0–5 to rate their self-perceived severity of 14 respiratory tract symptoms, including irritation of the nose, itchy nose, dry nose, burning nose, stuffy nose, running nose, itchy throat, irritation throat, swelling throat, burning throat, urge to cough, pressure on the chest, oppression to breath, and expectoration. A higher score indicates greater symptom severity, with a score of 0 referring to no symptoms and a score of 5 referring to a severe symptom. The total respiratory symptom score was then calculated by summing up the scores across the 14 symptoms. The questionnaires were completed half an hour before and immediately after each exposure session.</p></section><section id="s2.3.2"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Lung function test.</h4>
<p>Lung function was measured before and after each exposure session in a lab located on campus using a portable spirometer (Jaeger MasterscreenV5.01; CareFusion) under the instruction of the same trained operator. The lung function test was conducted according to the recommendation of the ATS/ERS.<sup><a href="#c24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c24">24</a></sup> Specifically, participants were instructed to make three attempts intermitted by at least 3 min and a qualified measure should have at least two acceptable and reproducible test results from all attempts. The measures of lung function included in this study were <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i37" display="inline" alttext="uppercase f e v begin subscript 1 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mtext>FEV</mtext></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, an indicator of airway obstruction; FVC, an indicator of airway restriction; and maximal mid-expiratory flow (MMEF), an indicator of small airway function. We also calculated the ratio of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i38" display="inline" alttext="uppercase f e v begin subscript 1 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mtext>FEV</mtext></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> and FVC (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i39" display="inline" alttext="uppercase f e v begin subscript 1 end subscript per uppercase f v c"><mrow><msub><mrow><mtext>FEV</mtext></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><mo>/</mo><mtext>FVC</mtext></mrow></math></span>) to examine airway obstruction.</p></section><section id="s2.3.3"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Sample collection and analysis.</h4>
<p>EBC and peripheral blood samples were collected before and after each exposure session within 1 h in a lab after returning to the campus. All participants were required to refrain from eating or drinking for at least 2 h before the testing and specimen collection. The EBC was collected by trained staff using an exhaled breath condensate collection system (Erich Jaeger GmbH) following the ATS/ERS recommended guidelines.<sup><a href="#c28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c28">28</a></sup> Participants were asked to rinse their mouths before sample collection to avoid saliva contamination. During the sample collection, participants were instructed to breathe into the collection system using tidal breathing to ensure that the volume of EBC was reproducible within individuals. The collection system runs at <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i40" display="inline" alttext="negative 5 degrees Celsius"><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>5</mn><mo>°</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></mrow></math></span> to condense water vapor in the exhaled breath. All EBC samples were stored at <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i41" display="inline" alttext="negative 80 degrees Celsius"><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>80</mn><mo>°</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></mrow></math></span> within 2 h after collection. Blood samples (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i42" display="inline" alttext="5 milliliters"><mrow><mn>5</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">mL</mi></mrow></math></span>) were collected by a professional medical staff member (a clinical nurse) using the serum separator tube (BD Vacutainer SST II advance) and were processed for serum extraction immediately after collection and then stored at <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i43" display="inline" alttext="negative 80 degrees Celsius"><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>80</mn><mo>°</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></mrow></math></span> until analysis.</p>
<p>Biomarkers in the EBC and blood samples collected before and after each exposure session were quantified using an enzyme linked immunosorbent assay (ELISA) according to the operating manual provided by the manufacturers. Specifically, serum surfactant proteins D (SP-D) (DSFPD0; R&amp;D Systems) and EBC ezrin (SEB297Hu; Cloud Clone Corp.) are indicators of lung epithelial injury, whereas EBC 8-isoprostane (516351; Cayman Chemical) and tumor necrosis <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i44" display="inline" alttext="factor lowercase alpha"><mrow><mtext>factor-</mtext><mi mathvariant="normal">α</mi></mrow></math></span> (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i45" display="inline" alttext="uppercase t n f lowercase alpha"><mrow><mtext>TNF-</mtext><mi mathvariant="normal">α</mi></mrow></math></span>) (HSTA00E; R&amp;D Systems) are markers of airway oxidative stress and inflammation, respectively. All samples collected from the same participant were processed in the same batch. All samples were analyzed twice according to the protocol of the manufacturer and a blank well was set in each batch and all samples (including the blank and standard controls). The measured value of each well was subtracted by the average value of the blank controls. Then means of two duplicated samples were used as the final measurement results in the statistical analysis. The coefficients of variation (CVs, %) for SP-D, 8-isoprostane, ezrin, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i46" display="inline" alttext="uppercase t n f lowercase alpha"><mrow><mtext>TNF-</mtext><mi mathvariant="normal">α</mi></mrow></math></span> detection were all <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i47" display="inline" alttext="less than 10 percent"><mrow><mo>&lt;</mo><mn>10</mn><mo>%</mo></mrow></math></span>. The limits of detection (LODs) for SP-D, 8-isoprostane, ezrin, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i48" display="inline" alttext="uppercase t n f lowercase alpha"><mrow><mtext>TNF-</mtext><mi mathvariant="normal">α</mi></mrow></math></span> detection were <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i49" display="inline" alttext="0.123 nanogram per milliliter"><mrow><mn>0.123</mn><mtext> ng</mtext><mo>/</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">mL</mi></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i50" display="inline" alttext="0.8 picogram per milliliter"><mrow><mn>0.8</mn><mtext> pg</mtext><mo>/</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">mL</mi></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i51" display="inline" alttext="0.312 nanogram per milliliter"><mrow><mn>0.312</mn><mtext> ng</mtext><mo>/</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">mL</mi></mrow></math></span>, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i52" display="inline" alttext="0.049 picogram per milliliter"><mrow><mn>0.049</mn><mtext> pg</mtext><mo>/</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">mL</mi></mrow></math></span>, respectively. Half of the LOD was used to replace those measurements below the LOD. Eventually, only measurements of two samples on ezrin and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i53" display="inline" alttext="uppercase t n f lowercase alpha"><mrow><mtext>TNF-</mtext><mi mathvariant="normal">α</mi></mrow></math></span> were below the LOD and were replaced.</p>
<p>EBC samples collected after each exposure session were used for metabolomic analysis in ultra-high performance liquid chromatography–mass spectrometry (UHPLC-MS/MS) (Vanquish; Thermo Fisher Scientific; and Orbitrap MS; Thermo). The detailed description on LC-MS/MS analysis can be found elsewhere.<sup><a href="#c29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c29">29</a></sup> In brief, LC-MS/MS analyses were performed using an UHPLC system (Vanquish; Thermo Fisher Scientific) with a UPLC BEH Amide column (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i54" display="inline" alttext="2.1 millimeters by 100 millimeters"><mrow><mn>2.1</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">mm</mi><mo>×</mo><mn>100</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">mm</mi></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i55" display="inline" alttext="1.7 micrometers"><mrow><mn>1.7</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi></mrow></math></span>) coupled to a Q Exactive HF-X (QE HFX) mass spectrometer (Orbitrap MS; Thermo). The QE HFX mass spectrometer was used owing to its ability to acquire MS/MS spectra in information-dependent acquisition mode in the control of the acquisition software (Xcalibur; Thermo). To validate the quality of measurements, quality control samples were generated by mixing aliquots of all samples with equal volume. The same pretreatment method was applied for quality control samples and samples to be tested. Pooled quality control samples were inserted in every several samples to assess the repeatability of the instrument. ProteoWizard was adopted to convert the raw data into the mzXML format and processed with a XCMS-based program for peak extraction. We excluded the features with missing values <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i56" display="inline" alttext="greater than 50 percent"><mrow><mo>&gt;</mo><mn>50</mn><mo>%</mo></mrow></math></span>. Half of the minimum value was used to replace each of the missing values. All data were Pareto-scaled before statistical analysis.</p></section></section><section id="s2.4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Statistical Analysis</h3>
<p>We first calculated the differences in respiratory system symptom scores, lung function measures, and concentrations of biomarkers (i.e., EBC ezrin, EBC 8-isoprostane, EBC <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i57" display="inline" alttext="uppercase t n f lowercase alpha"><mrow><mtext>TNF-</mtext><mi mathvariant="normal">α</mi></mrow></math></span>, and serum SP-D) before and after each exposure session (i.e., adjusting for the baseline). Then we applied linear mixed-effect (LME) models to estimate the associations of TRAP exposure with changes in each of the aforementioned health measures. Specifically, TRAP exposure was first fitted as a binary indicator of exposure session (0 for low-exposure session and 1 for high-exposure session) in the model to compare the difference of changes in these measures under the two exposure scenarios. In addition, we fitted separate models with continuous variables of air pollutants (UFP, BC, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i58" display="inline" alttext="particulate matter begin subscript 2.5 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">PM</mi></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>2.5</mn></mrow></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i59" display="inline" alttext="nitrogen dioxide"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">NO</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, or CO) at each exposure session (i.e., averages of pollutants concentrations over the 4-h exposure session) as the exposure of interest. For all models, we adjusted for demographic characteristics [i.e., age (continuous variable), sex (binary variable), and BMI (continuous variable)] to control for the potential heterogeneity between individuals, and meteorological conditions [i.e., temperature (continuous variable) and relative humidity (continuous variable)] to control for the potential heterogeneity between days. For biomarkers, we additionally adjusted for batch number in the fixed effect terms. A random intercept for each participant was also added into the model to account for within-participant correlations.<sup><a href="#c19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c19">19</a>,<a href="#c30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c30">30</a></sup> Last, we fitted nonlinear models for each pollutant using a natural spline function, adjusting for the same set of covariates. The likelihood ratio test was used to examine for possible nonlinear exposure–response relationships, and the results suggested no statistically significant nonlinear exposure–response relationship (Table S1). All analyses were implemented in R software (version 3.4.4; R Development Core Team) using the package lme4. Effect estimates for the biomarkers were expressed as mean absolute changes with 95% confidence intervals (CIs) associated with TRAP exposure and per interquartile range (IQR) increase of air pollutants.</p>
<p>The untargeted metabolome-wide association study (MWAS) was conducted for EBC metabolomics. We obtained the mass-to-charge ratio (<em>m/z</em>), retention time, and ion intensity for each detected metabolic feature. Features that were detected in <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i60" display="inline" alttext="less than 50 percent"><mrow><mo>&lt;</mo><mn>50</mn><mo>%</mo></mrow></math></span> of the samples were excluded. Ion intensities of the remaining features were log-transformed for normalization and were then added into separate LME models as dependent variables to examine their associations with TRAP exposure (binary) and with each pollutant (continuous),<sup><a href="#c31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c31">31</a></sup> adjusting for the same set of covariates. The Benjamini–Hochberg false discovery rate (FDR) was calculated to control for multiple comparisons, and an FDR of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i61" display="inline" alttext="less than 0.05"><mrow><mo>&lt;</mo><mn>0.05</mn></mrow></math></span> was considered statistically significant.</p>
<p>To identify the underlying biological pathways related to TRAP exposure, we used the mummichog pathway analysis (version 1.0.10; Python) for pathway enrichment analysis. Different from the traditional targeted metabolomic analysis strategy, the mummichog approach uses algorithms that leverage known metabolic pathways and networks to predict the function of each metabolite without identifying the metabolites <em>a priori</em>.<sup><a href="#c32" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c32">32</a></sup> This method has been commonly used in previous studies for predicting network activity from untargeted metabolomic analyses.<sup><a href="#c31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c31">31</a>,<a href="#c33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c33">33</a>–<a href="#c35" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c35">35</a></sup> We applied the mummichog approach to screen for the features that differ significantly between high-and low-TRAP exposure sessions or that were associated with air pollutants, under positive- and negative-ion modes, respectively. We applied Fisher’s exact test (FET) as an enrichment test of metabolic features on pathways, adjusting for type I error based on a method by Berriz et al.<sup><a href="#c36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c36">36</a></sup> An adjusted <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i62" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p less than 0.05"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>&lt;</mo><mn>0.05</mn></mrow></math></span> from FET was considered statistically significant. Metabolite features associated with enriched metabolic pathways were then annotated in the human metabolome database (HMDB), the METLIN database, and the Kyoto Encyclopedia of Genes and Genomes (KEGG) database.<sup><a href="#c37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c37">37</a></sup></p></section></section><section id="s3"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Results</h2>
<section id="s3.1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Descriptive Statistics</h3>
<p>As shown in <a href="#t1" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>, the mean <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i63" display="inline" alttext="plus or minus standard deviation"><mrow><mo>±</mo><mtext>standard deviation</mtext></mrow></math></span> (SD) of the age and BMI of participants were 23.5 <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i64" display="inline" alttext="plus or minus 2.4"><mrow><mo>±</mo><mn>2.4</mn></mrow></math></span> y and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i65" display="inline" alttext="21.8 plus or minus 2.9 kilograms per meter squared"><mrow><mn>21.8</mn><mo>±</mo><mn>2.9</mn><msup><mrow><mtext> </mtext><mi mathvariant="normal">kg</mi><mo>/</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></math></span>, respectively. Among the included 56 participants, 25 were males. No participant reported alcohol, medication and dietary supplement use, or exposure to smoking during the study period. During the road exposure session (i.e., high-TRAP exposure), the concentrations of UFP, BC, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i66" display="inline" alttext="nitrogen dioxide"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">NO</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, and CO were approximately two to three times higher than in the park exposure session (i.e., low-TRAP exposure), whereas <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i67" display="inline" alttext="particulate matter begin subscript 2.5 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">PM</mi></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>2.5</mn></mrow></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> concentration showed a relatively smaller difference, with only a 40% higher exposure in the road session compared with the park exposure session. Temperature and relative humidity were comparable in both sessions (<a href="#t2" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>). Spearman rank-correlation coefficients (<em>r</em>) between the five air pollutants ranged from 0.25 to 0.87 (Table S2).</p>

<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="TF2"><p>Note: BC, black carbon; CO, carbon monoxide; IQR, interquartile range; max, maximum; min, minimum; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i80" display="inline" alttext="nitrogen dioxide"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">NO</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, nitrogen dioxide; P25, 25th percentile; P50, 50th percentile; P75, 75th percentile; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i81" display="inline" alttext="particulate matter begin subscript 2.5 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">PM</mi></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>2.5</mn></mrow></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, fine particulate matter; SD, standard deviation; UFP, ultrafine particles.</p></div></div></section></section><section id="s3.2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Respiratory Symptoms in Relation to TRAP Exposure</h3>
<p>Overall, total respiratory symptom scores before the road and park exposure sessions were similar (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i82" display="inline" alttext="mean plus or minus standard deviation equals 1.70 plus or minus 2.28"><mrow><mtext>mean</mtext><mo>±</mo><mtext>SD</mtext><mo>=</mo><mn>1.70</mn><mo>±</mo><mn>2.28</mn></mrow></math></span> for the road exposure session and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i83" display="inline" alttext="1.89 plus or minus 3.00"><mrow><mn>1.89</mn><mo>±</mo><mn>3.00</mn></mrow></math></span> for the park exposure session, respectively; Table S3). At the end of each session, the mean total symptom score increased by 3.21 for the road exposure session; the scores for dry nose, itchy throat, and urge to cough had the largest increases. However, no notable increase in total score was found for the park exposure session. <a href="#t3" class="usa-link">Table 3</a> shows changes in the total score of respiratory symptoms related to TRAP and individual pollutants. Compared with changes in respiratory symptom scores before and after the park exposure session, we observed an increase of 2.615 (95% CI: 0.605, 4.626; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i84" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p equals 1.2 times 10 begin superscript negative 2 end superscript"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1.2</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mrow><mrow><mn>10</mn></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></mrow></msup></mrow></math></span>) in the changes in symptoms scores for the road exposure session. In addition, we found larger changes of symptom scores were associated with exposure to UFP (IQR: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i85" display="inline" alttext="16,934 particles per centimeter cubed"><mrow><mn>16,934</mn><msup><mrow><mtext> particles</mtext><mo>/</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">cm</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>3</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></math></span>; score difference per IQR increase: 1.793; 95% CI: 0.113, 3.474; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i86" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p equals 3.9 times 10 begin superscript negative 2 end superscript"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>3.9</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mrow><mrow><mn>10</mn></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></mrow></msup></mrow></math></span>), BC (IQR: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i87" display="inline" alttext="3 micrograms per meter cubed"><mrow><mn>3</mn><msup><mrow><mtext> </mtext><mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">g</mi><mo>/</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>3</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></math></span>; score per IQR increase difference: 2.917; 95% CI: 0.873, 4.960; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i88" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p equals 6.2 times 10 begin superscript negative 3 end superscript"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>6.2</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mrow><mrow><mn>10</mn></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow></mrow></msup></mrow></math></span>), and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i89" display="inline" alttext="nitrogen dioxide"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">NO</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> (IQR: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i90" display="inline" alttext="31 parts per billion"><mrow><mn>31</mn><mtext> ppb</mtext></mrow></math></span>; score difference per IQR increase: 3.360; 95% CI: 1.252, 5.467; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i91" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p equals 2.3 times 10 begin superscript negative 3 end superscript"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>2.3</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mrow><mrow><mn>10</mn></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow></mrow></msup></mrow></math></span>) than with exposure to CO (IQR: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i92" display="inline" alttext="632 parts per billion"><mrow><mn>632</mn><mtext> ppb</mtext></mrow></math></span>; score difference per IQR increase: 1.413; 95% CI: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i93" display="inline" alttext="negative 0.611"><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>0.611</mn></mrow></math></span>, 3.436; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i94" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p equals 1.7 times 10 begin superscript negative 1 end superscript"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1.7</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mrow><mrow><mn>10</mn></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></mrow></msup></mrow></math></span>) and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i95" display="inline" alttext="particulate matter begin subscript 2.5 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">PM</mi></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>2.5</mn></mrow></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> (IQR: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i96" display="inline" alttext="8 micrograms per meter cubed"><mrow><mn>8</mn><msup><mrow><mtext> </mtext><mi mathvariant="normal">μ</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">g</mi><mo>/</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>3</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></math></span>; score difference per IQR increase: 0.503; 95% CI: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i97" display="inline" alttext="negative 0.294"><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>0.294</mn></mrow></math></span>, 1.301; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i98" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p equals 2.2 times 10 begin superscript negative 1 end superscript"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>2.2</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mrow><mrow><mn>10</mn></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></mrow></msup></mrow></math></span>), respectively.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/t3/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="TF3"><p>Note: All the linear mixed-effect models were adjusted for age, sex, body mass index, batch number (only for biomarkers), and a random intercept for each participant and the previous 4-h moving average of temperature and relative humidity. IQR were calculated based on data measured at both high-and low-exposure sessions. BC, black carbon; CI, confidence interval; CO, carbon monoxide; EBC, exhaled breath condensate; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i256" display="inline" alttext="uppercase f e v begin subscript 1 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mtext>FEV</mtext></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, forced expiratory volume in the first second; FVC, forced vital capacity; IQR, interquartile range; MMEF, maximal mid-expiratory flow; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i257" display="inline" alttext="nitrogen dioxide"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">NO</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, nitrogen dioxide; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i258" display="inline" alttext="particulate matter begin subscript 2.5 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">PM</mi></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>2.5</mn></mrow></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, fine particulate matter; SP-D, surfactant proteins D; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i259" display="inline" alttext="uppercase t n f lowercase alpha"><mrow><mtext>TNF-</mtext><mi mathvariant="normal">α</mi></mrow></math></span>, tumor necrosis <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i260" display="inline" alttext="factor lowercase alpha"><mrow><mtext>factor-</mtext><mi mathvariant="normal">α</mi></mrow></math></span>; TRAP, traffic-related air pollution; UFP, ultrafine particles.</p></div></div></section></section><section id="s3.3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">TRAP Exposure and Lung Function</h3>
<p>We found no notable differences in lung function measures before the road and park exposure sessions (<a href="#t4" class="usa-link">Table 4</a>). Compared with those measured before the exposure sessions, we observed lower FVC, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i261" display="inline" alttext="uppercase f e v begin subscript 1 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mtext>FEV</mtext></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, and MMEF in our participants after the road exposure session, whereas no drastic changes were found between all lung function measures before and after the park exposure session. After adjusting for all covariates in LME models, we found TRAP exposure was associated with lower <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i262" display="inline" alttext="uppercase f e v begin subscript 1 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mtext>FEV</mtext></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> and MMEF (<a href="#t3" class="usa-link">Table 3</a>). For example, compared with lung function changes in the park exposure session, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i263" display="inline" alttext="uppercase f e v begin subscript 1 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mtext>FEV</mtext></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> and MMEF decreased by <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i264" display="inline" alttext="0.075 liter"><mrow><mn>0.075</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">L</mi></mrow></math></span> (95% CI: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i265" display="inline" alttext="negative 0.138"><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>0.138</mn></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i266" display="inline" alttext="negative 0.012"><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>0.012</mn></mrow></math></span>; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i267" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p equals 2.1 times 10 begin superscript negative 2 end superscript"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>2.1</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mrow><mrow><mn>10</mn></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></mrow></msup></mrow></math></span>) and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i268" display="inline" alttext="0.190 liter per second"><mrow><mn>0.190</mn><mspace width="0.3em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">L</mi><mo>/</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi></mrow></math></span> (95% CI: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i269" display="inline" alttext="negative 0.351"><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>0.351</mn></mrow></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i270" display="inline" alttext="negative 0.029"><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>0.029</mn></mrow></math></span>; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i271" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p equals 2.4 times 10 begin superscript negative 2 end superscript"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>2.4</mn><mo>×</mo><msup><mrow><mrow><mn>10</mn></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></mrow></msup></mrow></math></span>) after the road exposure session.</p>

In addition, the associations between biomarkers and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i369" display="inline" alttext="particulate matter begin subscript 2.5 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">PM</mi></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>2.5</mn></mrow></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> exposure showed null.</p></section><section id="s3.5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">TRAP Exposure and Airway Metabolome</h3>
<p>In total, we detected 2,305 and 2,098 metabolite features from positive- and negative-ion modes, respectively. Among them, 936 (positive mode) and 945 (negative mode) metabolic features, respectively, were identified as differential metabolic features between the road and park exposure sessions. The mummichog pathway analysis further identified perturbations in 23 and 32 metabolic pathways based on these features under the positive- and negative-ion modes, respectively (<a href="#f1" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>; Table S4). These enriched metabolic pathways were closely related to the systemic inflammatory response (e.g., arginine and proline metabolism, and leukotriene metabolism), oxidative stress (e.g., linoleate metabolism, purine metabolism, methionine and cysteine metabolism, and vitamin E metabolism), and energy metabolism [e.g., tricarboxylic acid (TCA) cycle].</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="f1" title="Figure 1 is a set of two heatmaps titled positive ionization mode and negative ionization mode, plotting Arginine and Proline Metabolism, Aspartate and asparagine metabolism, Beta-Alanine metabolism, Butanoate metabolism, Carnitine shuttle, Di-unsaturated fatty acid beta-oxidation, Fatty acid activation, Fatty Acid Metabolism, Glycine, serine, alanine and threonine metabolism, Glycosphingolipid metabolism, Linoleate metabolism, Lysine metabolism, Nitrogen metabolism, Omega-3 fatty acid metabolism, Pentose and Glucuronate Interconversions, Saturated fatty acids beta-oxidation, Squalene and cholesterol biosynthesis, Tyrosine metabolism, Urea cycle or amino group metabolism, Vitamin B3 (nicotinate and nicotinamide) metabolism, Vitamin B6 (pyridoxine) metabolism, Vitamin B9 (folate) metabolism, Vitamin E metabolism; and Alanine and Aspartate Metabolism, Arginine and Proline Metabolism, Ascorbate (Vitamin C) and Aldarate Metabolism, Beta-Alanine metabolism, Butanoate metabolism, Chondroitin sulfate degradation, De novo fatty acid biosynthesis, Fatty acid activation, Fatty Acid Metabolism, Fructose and mannose metabolism, Glutamate metabolism, Glycerophospholipid metabolism, Glycolysis and Gluconeogenesis, Glycosphingolipid metabolism, Glyoxylate and Dicarboxylate Metabolism, Heparan sulfate degradation, Hexose phosphorylation, Leukotriene metabolism, Lysine metabolism, Methionine and cysteine metabolism, Pentose phosphate pathway, Phosphatidylinositol phosphate metabolism, Propanoate metabolism, Purine metabolism, Pyrimidine metabolism, Pyruvate Metabolism, Sialic acid metabolism, TCA cycle, Tryptophan metabolism, Tyrosine metabolism, Urea cycle or amino group metabolism, Vitamin B9 (folate) metabolism (y-axis) across T R A P, U F P, B C, N O 2, C O, and particulate matter begin subscript 2.5 end subscript (x-axis), respectively. A scale depicting lowercase p values ranges from 0.00 to 1.00 in increments of 0.05."><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 1.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7e99/10159268/5854e06d8d67/ehp11139_f1.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1329" width="4260" alt="Figure 1 is a set of two heatmaps titled positive ionization mode and negative ionization mode, plotting Arginine and Proline Metabolism, Aspartate and asparagine metabolism, Beta-Alanine metabolism, Butanoate metabolism, Carnitine shuttle, Di-unsaturated fatty acid beta-oxidation, Fatty acid activation, Fatty Acid Metabolism, Glycine, serine, alanine and threonine metabolism, Glycosphingolipid metabolism, Linoleate metabolism, Lysine metabolism, Nitrogen metabolism, Omega-3 fatty acid metabolism, Pentose and Glucuronate Interconversions, Saturated fatty acids beta-oxidation, Squalene and cholesterol biosynthesis, Tyrosine metabolism, Urea cycle or amino group metabolism, Vitamin B3 (nicotinate and nicotinamide) metabolism, Vitamin B6 (pyridoxine) metabolism, Vitamin B9 (folate) metabolism, Vitamin E metabolism; and Alanine and Aspartate Metabolism, Arginine and Proline Metabolism, Ascorbate (Vitamin C) and Aldarate Metabolism, Beta-Alanine metabolism, Butanoate metabolism, Chondroitin sulfate degradation, De novo fatty acid biosynthesis, Fatty acid activation, Fatty Acid Metabolism, Fructose and mannose metabolism, Glutamate metabolism, Glycerophospholipid metabolism, Glycolysis and Gluconeogenesis, Glycosphingolipid metabolism, Glyoxylate and Dicarboxylate Metabolism, Heparan sulfate degradation, Hexose phosphorylation, Leukotriene metabolism, Lysine metabolism, Methionine and cysteine metabolism, Pentose phosphate pathway, Phosphatidylinositol phosphate metabolism, Propanoate metabolism, Purine metabolism, Pyrimidine metabolism, Pyruvate Metabolism, Sialic acid metabolism, TCA cycle, Tryptophan metabolism, Tyrosine metabolism, Urea cycle or amino group metabolism, Vitamin B9 (folate) metabolism (y-axis) across T R A P, U F P, B C, N O 2, C O, and particulate matter begin subscript 2.5 end subscript (x-axis), respectively. <div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/f1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>The identified metabolic pathways associated with TRAP and individual air pollutants in positive-ionization mode (POS) and negative-ionization mode (NEG) based on the untargeted metabolome-wide association study (MWAS) conducted for exhaled breath condensate (EBC) metabolomics in a randomized crossover trial in China (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i370" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic n equals 56"><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>56</mn></mrow></math></span> adults). TRAP exposure was fitted as a binary indicator of exposure (low/high); individual pollutants were modeled as continuous variables. The linear mixed-effect models and the mummichog pathway analysis (version 1.0.10; Python) were applied for pathway enrichment analysis. Fisher’s exact test (FET) as an enrichment test of metabolic features on pathways was applied, and an adjusted <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i371" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p less than 0.05"><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo>&lt;</mo><mn>0.05</mn></mrow></math></span> from FET was considered statistically significant. <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i372" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic p"><mi>p</mi></math></span>-Values are shown in Table S4. Note: BC, black carbon; CO, carbon monoxide; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i373" display="inline" alttext="nitrogen dioxide"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">NO</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, nitrogen dioxide; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i374" display="inline" alttext="particulate matter begin subscript 2.5 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">PM</mi></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>2.5</mn></mrow></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, fine particulate matter; TCA, tricarboxylic acid; TRAP, traffic-related air pollution; UFP, ultrafine particles.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Among these enriched metabolic pathways, 156 and 129 unique metabolic features were associated with TRAP air pollutants in the positive- and negative-ionization modes, respectively. Based on the enriched metabolic pathways, we further confirmed 27 of them were associated with TRAP (<a href="#t5" class="usa-link">Table 5</a>). Most of these features were endogenous metabolites related to inflammatory, oxidative stress, and energy metabolism. For example, we found metabolites related to inflammation and oxidative stress, including leukotriene <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i375" display="inline" alttext="uppercase b begin subscript 4 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>4</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, 13-hydroxyoctadecadienoic acid, hypoxanthine, uric acid, and <span class="font-variant-small-caps">l</span>-arginine, increased after the road exposure session and with individual pollutants. Meanwhile, metabolites that were suggested to have anti-inflammation and anti-oxidative stress properties (e.g., <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i376" display="inline" alttext="gamma-linolenic acid"><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">γ</mi><mtext>-linolenic acid</mtext></mrow></math></span>) were found to be reduced after the road exposure session. Moreover, metabolites related to energy metabolism (e.g., fumarate, succinate, <span class="font-variant-small-caps">d</span>-glucose, lactate, pyruvate) were also found to increase after the road exposure session and with higher UFP and BC concentrations.</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="t5"><h4 class="obj_head">Table 5.</h4>

/, An association (effect) of the metabolites related to the pollutant or TRAPs is not identified in pathway analysis; 13-HODE, 13-hydroxyoctadecadienoic acid; 13-HPODE, 13-hydroperxyoctadecadienoic acid; 13-OxoODE, 13-oxooctadecadienoic acid; BC, black carbon; Br, bromine; CO, carbon monoxide; ESI, electron spray ionization; H, hydrogen; K, potassium; Na, sodium; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i575" display="inline" alttext="nitrogen dioxide"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi display="inline" alttext="particulate matter begin subscript 2.5 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">PM</mi></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>2.5</mn></mrow></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, fine particulate matter; TCA, tricarboxylic acid; TRAP, traffic-related air pollution; UFP, ultrafine particles.</p></div></div></section></section></section><section id="s4"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Discussion</h2>
<p>In this study, we conducted a randomized crossover trial to explore respiratory responses related to TRAP exposure among healthy adults. After comparing changes before and after the road and park exposure sessions, we found TRAP exposure was associated with higher respiratory symptom scores and lower lung function. Further, biomarkers related to lung epithelial injury, airway inflammation, and oxidative stress increased after exposure to TRAP. Metabolomics analysis in EBC samples identified metabolic signals and pathways closely related to inflammation, oxidative stress, and energy metabolism (<a href="#f2" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>). We also found UFP, BC, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i577" display="inline" alttext="nitrogen dioxide"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">NO</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, and CO, but not <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i578" display="inline" alttext="particulate matter begin subscript 2.5 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">PM</mi></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>2.5</mn></mrow></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, were significantly associated with changes in respiratory health markers, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i579" display="inline" alttext="nitrogen dioxide"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">NO</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> had stronger associations with changes in respiratory symptom scores and lung function measures.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="f2" title="Figure 2 is an illustration flowchart with six steps. Step 1: traffic-related air pollution leads to airway epithelial cells. Step 2: Epithelial cells lead to energy metabolic rewiring, pro-inflammation tumor necrosis factor lowercase a, and oxidative stress (8-isoprostane). Step 3: Under energy metabolic rewiring, Glycolysis and Gluconeogenesis, including glucose, glucose-6-phosphate, pyruvate, and lactate, are combined with the Tricarboxylic Acid Cycle, including malate, fumarate, and succinate. Under pro-inflammation, tumor necrosis factor lowercase a, arginine, and proline metabolism are involved, including L-arginine with inducible nitric oxide synthase, citrulline, nitric oxide, and leukotrienes, including leukotriene B-4 and 10,11-dihydro-leukotriene B 4. Under Oxidative stress 8-isoprostane, linoleate, 13- hydroperxyoctadecadienoic acid, 13-hydroxyoctadecadienoic acid, 13-oxooctadecadienoic acid; Purine metabolism, including hypoxanthine with Xanthine Oxidoreductase, xanthine with Xanthine Oxidoreductase, uric acid; Methionine and cysteine metabolism, including methionine; and Vitamin E metabolism, including alpha-tocotrienol and 11 prime carboxy-alpha-tocotrienol. Purine metabolism with methionine and cysteine metabolism leads to reactive oxygen species. The reactive oxygen species lead to cell membrane phospholipid. The cell membrane phospholipid and phospholipase A2 lead to free fatty acids. The free fatty acids lead to leukotriene. Step 4: Lung epithelial injury, including ezrin and surfactant proteins D. Step 5: Lung function reductions, including forced vital capacity, forced expiratory volume in the first second, ratio of forced expiratory volume in the first second to forced vital capacity, and maximal mid-expiratory flow. Step 6: Respiratory symptoms, including symptom scores."><h3 class="obj_head">Figure 2.</h3>
