<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/f2/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Possible mechanisms underlying the respiratory effects of TRAP exposure identified in this randomized crossover study in China (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i580" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic n equals 56"><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>56</mn></mrow></math></span> adults). Note: 13-HODE, 13-hydroxyoctadecadienoic acid; 13-HPODE, 13-hydroperxyoctadecadienoic acid; 13-OxoODE, 13-oxooctadecadienoic acid; EBC, exhaled breath condensate; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i581" display="inline" alttext="uppercase f a d h begin subscript 2 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mtext>FADH</mtext></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, reduced form of flavin adenine dinucleotide; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i582" display="inline" alttext="uppercase f e v begin subscript 1 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mtext>FEV</mtext></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>, forced expiratory volume in the first second; FVC, forced vital capacity; iNOS, inducible nitric oxide synthase; MMEF, maximal mid-expiratory flow; NADPH, reduced form of nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate; p, phosphate; PLA2, phospholipase A2; ROS, reactive oxygen species; SP-D, surfactant proteins D; TCA, tricarboxylic acid; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i583" display="inline" alttext="uppercase t n f lowercase alpha"><mrow><mtext>TNF-</mtext><mi mathvariant="normal">α</mi></mrow></math></span>, tumor necrosis <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i584" display="inline" alttext="factor lowercase alpha"><mrow><mtext>factor-</mtext><mi mathvariant="normal">α</mi></mrow></math></span>; TRAP, traffic-related air pollution; XOR, xanthine oxidoreductase.</p></figcaption></figure><section id="s4.1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Respiratory Symptoms and Lung Function Changes Related to TRAP Exposure</h3>
<p>Previous epidemiological studies have found that exposure to TRAP was associated with respiratory symptoms.<sup><a href="#c38" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c38">38</a>–<a href="#c40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c40">40</a></sup> However, those studies were mostly observational and focused on vulnerable populations (e.g., children, asthmatics) in Europe. Therefore, it may not be feasible to directly compare their results with our findings. A previous randomized, crossover trial in the UK assessed the effects on respiratory and cardiovascular responses of TRAP exposure among healthy elderly and elderly with chronic lung or heart disease, and it found short-term exposure to TRAP was associated with symptoms in the respiratory tract (e.g., cough, sputum, wheeze) and lower lung function.<sup><a href="#c18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c18">18</a></sup> Consistent with our results, that UK study also illustrated that the benefits of walking exercise on lung function might be offset by TRAP exposure among adults.<sup><a href="#c18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c18">18</a></sup></p>
<p>Similarly, we found a significant decrease in lung function following acute TRAP exposure. These lung function changes indicate airway obstruction (manifested by a significant decrease in <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i585" display="inline" alttext="uppercase f e v begin subscript 1 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mtext>FEV</mtext></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> and no significant change in FVC) and small airway changes (manifested by a significant decrease in MMEF). MMEF has been considered a sensitive physiological marker of small airway function decline.<sup><a href="#c41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c41">41</a></sup> Therefore, the observed declined MMEF associated with TRAP exposure suggests TRAP exposure might be associated with early pathophysiological impairment of small airways among healthy adults.<sup><a href="#c42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c42">42</a></sup></p></section><section id="s4.2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Potential Biological Mechanisms of TRAP-Related Respiratory Effect</h3>
<p>We found significant changes of serum SP-D and EBC ezrin associated with TRAP exposure. Both serum SP-D and EBC ezrin are biomarkers of airway epithelial damage. SP-D regulate airway surface tension.<sup><a href="#c43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c43">43</a>,<a href="#c44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c44">44</a></sup> Previous studies in humans have suggested SP-D were inversely associated with lung function.<sup><a href="#c45" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c45">45</a>,<a href="#c46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c46">46</a></sup> Ezrin is related to the integrity of the airway epithelial barrier by maintaining the normal morphology and intercellular adhesion of epithelial cells.<sup><a href="#c47" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c47">47</a>,<a href="#c48" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c48">48</a></sup> We hypothesize that the increase of serum SP-D and decrease of EBC ezrin after the road exposure session suggests that TRAP exposure might compromise the alveolar epithelial integrity and impair airway epithelial lining, which can further impair lung function and lead to respiratory symptoms.</p>
<p>Our airway metabolomic analysis showed that TRAP exposure was associated with increased metabolites in oxidative stress pathways, including hypoxanthine, uric acid, linoleate, 13-hydroxyoctadecadienoic acid (13-HODE), and methionine. Hypoxanthine was oxidized to xanthine and uric acid by xanthine oxidase, and this process could produce reactive oxygen species (ROS).<sup><a href="#c49" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c49">49</a></sup> ROS can further activate lipid oxidation phospholipase A2, which then hydrolyzes phospholipids to polyunsaturated free fatty acids (e.g., linoleic acid).<sup><a href="#c50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c50">50</a></sup> Linoleic acid can be converted into 13-hydroperoxyoctadecadenoic acid (13-HPODE) and 13-HODE by lipoxygenases, and the latter is a potential biomarker for oxidative stress-affecting lipids.<sup><a href="#c50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c50">50</a></sup> In this study, we also consistently found that TRAP exposure was associated with higher levels of EBC 8-isoprostane, a biomarker of oxidative stress,<sup><a href="#c51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c51">51</a></sup> further supporting the elevated oxidative stress associated with TRAP exposure.</p>
<p>In addition, our data also linked TRAP exposure with two inflammation-related pathways, the leukotriene metabolism and the arginine and proline metabolism. Consistently, we found higher levels of EBC leukotriene <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i586" display="inline" alttext="uppercase b begin subscript 4 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>4</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> (a lipid molecule with pro-inflammatory properties) and a slight increase of EBC <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i587" display="inline" alttext="uppercase t n f lowercase alpha"><mrow><mtext>TNF-</mtext><mi mathvariant="normal">α</mi></mrow></math></span> after exposure to high levels of TRAP, indicating that TRAP exposure can increase airway inflammation.<sup><a href="#c52" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c52">52</a></sup></p>
<p>We also observed higher EBC glucose, pyruvate, lactate, succinate, and fumarate after exposure to TRAP. These metabolites are related to energy metabolism, which is essential to cell function and survival. More specifically, the observed increases in these metabolites might suggest up-regulated energy metabolism and activated anaerobic glycolysis in airway epithelial cells. It is possible that energy metabolic rewiring is a potential pathway leading to airway epithelial injury. Consistent with our findings, a recent animal study also found an increased metabolic rearrangement from the TCA cycle to glycolysis as a manifestation of metabolic alteration in lung tissue after <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i588" display="inline" alttext="particulate matter begin subscript 2.5 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">PM</mi></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>2.5</mn></mrow></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> exposure.<sup><a href="#c53" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c53">53</a></sup></p></section><section id="s4.3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Pollutant-Specific Associations with the Outcomes</h3>
<p>We found that <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i589" display="inline" alttext="particulate matter begin subscript 2.5 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">PM</mi></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>2.5</mn></mrow></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> concentration had a smaller difference between the road and park exposure sessions compared with the other pollutants, which is consistent with previous findings.<sup><a href="#c18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c18">18</a>,<a href="#c54" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c54">54</a></sup> Unlike the other pollutants, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i590" display="inline" alttext="particulate matter begin subscript 2.5 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">PM</mi></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>2.5</mn></mrow></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> can be from both local sources and regional transportation.<sup><a href="#c55" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c55">55</a></sup> In this study, traffic is the main factor contributing to the differential levels of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i591" display="inline" alttext="particulate matter begin subscript 2.5 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">PM</mi></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>2.5</mn></mrow></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> exposure between the road and park sessions. Therefore, when regional transportation dominates, the differences in <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i592" display="inline" alttext="particulate matter begin subscript 2.5 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">PM</mi></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>2.5</mn></mrow></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> concentrations contributed by the local sources may be masked by the background level.<sup><a href="#c56" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c56">56</a></sup></p>
<p>Among all the pollutants considered in the analysis, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i593" display="inline" alttext="nitrogen dioxide"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">NO</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> appeared to have the strongest associations with lung function, especially with decreased MMEF, suggesting <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i594" display="inline" alttext="nitrogen dioxide"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">NO</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> may impair small airway function. Unlike the other water-soluble gaseous pollutants, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i595" display="inline" alttext="nitrogen dioxide"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">NO</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> hydrolyzes slowly in the airway and can reach bronchioles and alveoli and cause mucous edema in the small airways.<sup><a href="#c57" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c57">57</a>–<a href="#c59" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c59">59</a></sup> In addition, we found BC and UFP were associated with lung function decline, which was consistent with previous findings in London.<sup><a href="#c18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c18">18</a>,<a href="#c19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c19">19</a></sup> We found BC and UFP were also associated with changes of biomarkers and metabolic pathways related to oxidative stress and inflammation. Previous studies have reported similar associations of these two pollutants from traffic sources with oxidative stress and inflammation in the respiratory system.<sup><a href="#c60" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c60">60</a>,<a href="#c61" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c61">61</a></sup> We did not observe significant associations of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i596" display="inline" alttext="particulate matter begin subscript 2.5 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">PM</mi></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>2.5</mn></mrow></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> with any outcome measures, which is in line with prior findings based on the same study design.<sup><a href="#c18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c18">18</a>,<a href="#c19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c19">19</a></sup> One possible reason for the null associations of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i597" display="inline" alttext="particulate matter begin subscript 2.5 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">PM</mi></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>2.5</mn></mrow></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> might be that the difference in <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i598" display="inline" alttext="particulate matter begin subscript 2.5 end subscript"><mrow><msub><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">PM</mi></mrow></mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>2.5</mn></mrow></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span> exposure levels between the road and the park groups was small.</p></section><section id="s4.4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Strengths and Limitations</h3>
<p>The randomized crossover trial design of this study can effectively minimize the possibility of unmeasured confounding. Moreover, the combination of the subclinical indicators and the omics technique allowed us to comprehensively investigate the global molecular responses of the respiratory system related to TRAP exposure. Moreover, our metabolomic analysis was based on the EBC samples, which can reflect the local changes in the respiratory tract after exposure to TRAP.</p>
<p>Our study also has several limitations. First, although we controlled for multiple confounders through the randomized crossover study design,<sup><a href="#c62" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c62">62</a>,<a href="#c63" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="c63">63</a></sup> residual confounding was still possible under the complex real-life environment. For example, without blinding, participants may differentially report respiratory symptoms between these two exposure sessions. In addition, although we considered only participants who lived on the same campus for at least 1 y to reduce the influence of long-term air pollution exposure, early life air pollution exposure (e.g., exposure during childhood) may have long-term impacts on lung function. Second, all outcome measurements were conducted only once right after each exposure session. Therefore, the delayed effects of TRAP exposure on respiratory health were not considered. Finally, although the effects of the single traffic-related pollutant were examined, our ability to identify the independent effects of each pollutant was limited by the strong correlation (Spearman <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="i599" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic r equals 0.25 to 0.87"><mrow><mi>r</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.25</mn><mo>–</mo><mn>0.87</mn></mrow></math></span>) among these pollutants. Only healthy young adults were included in this study; therefore, our findings may not be generalizable to other susceptible populations with preexisting conditions.</p></section></section><section id="s5"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Conclusion</h2>
<p>In this randomized crossover study, we found short-term exposure to TRAP exposure was associated with respiratory symptoms and airway impairment. Airway biomarkers and EBC metabolomic analysis suggested TRAP exposure can lead to lung epithelial injury, airway inflammation, oxidative stress, and energy metabolism disturbance. These findings provide evidence for the adverse respiratory health effects of TRAP exposure and insights into the potential biological mechanisms.</p></section><section id="sec27"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Supplementary Material</h2>
